Divided powers and derived De Rham cohomology
Kirill Magidson

Abstract
We develop the formalism of derived divided power algebras, and revisit the theory of derived De Rham and derived crystalline cohomology in this framework. We characterize derived De Rham cohomology of a derived commutative algebra A A over a base R R , together with the Hodge filtration on it, in terms of the universal property as the largest filtered divided power thickening of A A . We show that our approach recovers the classical De Rham cohomology in the case of a smooth map R → A R\rightarrow A , and therefore in general, recovers the derived De Rham cohomology in the sense of Illusie. Along the way, we develop some generalities on square-zero extensions and derivations in derived algebraic geometry and apply them to give the universal property of the first Hodge truncation of the derived De Rham cohomology. Finally, we define derived crystalline cohomology relative to a general divided power base, show that it satisfies the main properties of the crystalline cohomology and coincides with the classical crystalline cohomology in the smooth case.



1
Introduction and motivation.

1.1
Crystalline cohomology and De Rham cohomology.

Assume that
A
is a commutative algebra, smooth over some field
k
. The
De Rham cohomology
of
A
is a sequence of
k
-vector spaces
H^{n}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(A/k)
defined as the cohomology of the
De Rham complex
of
A

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{{\mathrm{dR}}}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{A/k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{{\mathrm{dR}}}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{A/k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\;\;...\;\;,}

where
\Omega^{n}_{A/k}:=\Lambda^{n}_{A}(\Omega^{1}_{A/k})
is the
n
-th exterior power of the
A
-module of Kahler differentials of
A
relative to
k
, and
d_{dR}
is the De Rham differential, i.e. the unique differential extending the universal
R
-linear derivation of
A
,
d_{{\mathrm{dR}}}:A\rightarrow\Omega^{1}_{A/k}
and satisfying the graded Leibnitz rule. The importance of De Rham cohomology in algebraic geometry comes from the fact when
k=\mathbb{C}
, De Rham cohomology computes the singular cohomology of the topological space of complex points of
\Spec(A)
. However, over a field of positive characteristic, De Rham cohomology is a less satisfactory invariant, as it consists purely of torsion, and one can not recover characteristic
0
information from it. Instead, the theory of
crystalline cohomology
comes into play.

The genesis of crystalline cohomology theory started with a deeper understanding of the De Rham cohomology. In the seminal paper,
[
13
]
A.Grothendieck found a remarkable geometric interpretation of De Rham cohomology as the cohomology of a certain site. Assume first that
A
is smooth over a field of characteristic
0
. The
infinitesimal site
of
\Spec(A)
is the category of pairs
(U,Z)
where
U
is an open affine subscheme of
\Spec(A)
and
Z
is a nilpotent thickening of
U
. The infinitesimal site has a structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}
whose value on the pair
(U,Z)
is the algebra of functions
\Gamma(Z,\mathcal{O}_{Z})
. Grothendieck showed that over a field of characteristic zero, the De Rham cohomology is equivalent to the cohomology of the structure sheaf of the infinitesimal site.

Now assume
A
is defined over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Based on the observation that in characteristic
0
De Rham cohomology computes the cohomology of the infinitesimal site, Grothendieck defined a new theory of
crystalline cohomology
as the cohomology of the so-called
crystalline site
. The definition of the crystalline site is similar to the definition of the infinitesimal site, in the sense that both sites parametrize certain thickenings of open affine subscheme of
\Spec(A)
. However, a crucial change is implemented in the crystalline case: instead of merely nilpotent thickenings, one now considers
divided power thickenings
. Let us recall what it means. Assume
I\subset A
is an ideal in a ring
A
. A
divided power structure
on
I
is a sequence of maps
\gamma_{n}:I\rightarrow A
for
n\geq 1
satisfying a number of relations ensuring that
\gamma_{n}(x)
behaves like
\frac{x^{n}}{n!}
for any
x\in I
and
n\geq 1
. Over a field of characteristic
0
, any ideal has unique divided powers. But over other base rings, there can be many different divided power structures on an ideal, or none at all. The
crystalline site
of
\Spec(A)
is the category whose objects are triples
(U,Z,\gamma)
where
U
is an open affine subscheme of
\Spec(A)
,
Z
is a nilpotent thickening of
U
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with a divided power structure
\gamma
on the ideal defining the closed embedding
U\subset Z
, where divided powers are required to be compatible with the divided powers on the ideal
(p)\subset\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. The scheme
Z
is referred to as a
“divided power thickening”
of
U
. The crystalline site has a crystalline structure sheaf which takes any triple
(U,Z,\gamma)
to the algebra of global functions
\Gamma(Z,\mathcal{O}_{Z})
on
Z
. The
crystalline cohomology

H^{n}_{{\mathrm{crys}}}(A/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is the cohomology of the structure sheaf of the crystalline site.

It turns out that the crystalline cohomology defined as the cohomology of the crystalline site, is closely related to the De Rham cohomology defined in terms of differential forms. A comparison theorem of Grothendieck says that after base change to
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, the crystalline cohomology groups are canonically isomorphic to the
De Rham cohomology
groups of
A
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
:

H^{n}_{{\mathrm{crys}}}(A/\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\simeq H^{n}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(A/\mathbb{F}_{p}).

(1.1)

Another statement concerns the presence of a smooth lift of
\Spec(A)
to a formally smooth
p
-adic scheme
\Spec\widetilde{A}
. In this situation, the crystalline cohomology of
A
can be computed as
p
-adically completed De Rham cohomology of the lift:

H^{n}_{{\mathrm{crys}}}(A/\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq H^{n}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\widetilde{A}/\mathbb{Z})_{p}^{\widehat{\;\;}}.

(1.2)

These statements can seem surprising at first glance. The right side of Equations
1.1
and
1.2
are defined in very different terms than the left side. The De Rham cohomology is defined as the cohomology of a concrete commutative differential graded algebra. On the other hand, the crystalline cohomology is defined in terms of divided power algebras rather than commutative differential graded algebras. The equivalences
1.1
and
1.2
suggest that there should be a close relationship between commutative differential graded algebras and divided power algebras. This is further affirmed by a classical computation due to L.Illusie
[
16
]
. The formation of De Rham complex

(A/R)\longmapsto(\Omega^{\bullet}_{A/R},d_{{\mathrm{dR}}})

on the category of smooth
R
-algebras can be derived to all
R
-algebras to obtain the theory of
the derived De Rham complex
developed by L.Illusie in the foundational texts
[
15
,
16
]
. The derived De Rham complex assigns to any commutative
R
-algebra
A
a commutative algebra object
|\mathbb{L}\Omega^{\bullet}_{A/R}|
in the derived category
\mathrm{D}(R)
of
R
-modules. Assume
R\rightarrow A
is a surjective map and the ideal
I=\ker(R\rightarrow A)
is generated by a regular sequence. Then it is shown in
[
16
]
that there is an equivalence

|\mathbb{L}\Omega^{\bullet}_{A/R}|\simeq\Env^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}(I),

(1.3)

where
\Env^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}(I)
is the
divided power envelope
of
R
at the ideal
I
, i.e. the algebra obtained by freely adjoining to
R
all divided powers of generators of the ideal
I
. The equivalence
1.3
respect an additional piece of structure present on both sides, namely, a filtration. For a smooth map
R\rightarrow A
, the De Rham complex
\Omega_{A/R}^{\bullet}
has a multiplicative
Hodge filtration
whose
n
-th stage is the subcomplex

\Omega^{\geq n}_{A/R}:=0\rightarrow 0\rightarrow...\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\Omega^{n}_{A/R}\rightarrow\Omega_{A/R}^{n+1}\rightarrow...

In other words, the subcomplex
\Omega^{\geq n}_{A/R}\subset\Omega^{\bullet}_{A/R}
is spanned by differential forms
a_{0}da_{1}\wedge da_{2}\wedge...\wedge da_{i}\in\Omega_{A/R}^{i}
of degree
i\geq n
. Deriving this construction, we obtain a
“derived”
Hodge filtration
\mathbb{L}\Omega^{\geq\star}_{A/R}
and thereby provides us a with functor
\mathbb{L}\Omega_{-/R}:\CAlg_{R,\heartsuit}\rightarrow\Fil^{\geq 0}\CAlg_{R}
, where the target is the
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra objects in the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
of non-negatively filtered
R
-modules.

The derived Hodge filtration has a counterpart on the divided power side; for a surjective map
R\rightarrow A
with regular ideal
I
, the divided power envelope
\Env^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}(I)
carries a filtration
\Env^{\mathrm{pd},\geq\star}_{R}(I)
whose
n
-th stage is spanned by divided power monomials
\gamma_{i_{1}}(x_{1})....\gamma_{i_{k}}(x_{k})
with
i_{1}+...+i_{k}\geq n
for
x_{i}\in I
.

A classical computation of L.Illusie
[
16
, Corollary 1.4.4.1]
states that the equivalence
1.3
does in fact refine to a filtered equivalence

\mathbb{L}\Omega^{\geq\star}_{A/R}\simeq\Env_{R}^{\mathrm{pd},\geq\star}(I).

(1.4)

This paper is an attempt to develop a formalism in which the equivalence
1.4
becomes obvious by giving a universal property of the derived De Rham cohomology in the world of divided power algebras. Such a theory ought to make various crystalline comparisons conceptually simpler. Another expectation from such a theory, is that it should make more geometrically clear the relation between derived De Rham cohomology and the stacky approach via
the De Rham stack
developed recently by Drinfeld and Bhatt-Lurie in the texts
[
9
]
,
[
2
]
,
[
5
]
.

Let us now outline our approach to derived De Rham cohomology derived via divided power algebras. Fix a base commutative ring
R
, and let
A
be a commutative
R
-algebra. The
De Rham complex of
A
relative to
R
is a differential graded
R
-algebra
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{A/R},d_{{\mathrm{dR}}})
which has the following universal property. Assume
(B^{\bullet},d)
is another differential graded
R
-algebra endowed with a commutative
R
-algebra map
f^{0}:A\rightarrow B^{0}
. Then
f^{0}
uniquely extends to a differential graded
R
-algebra map
f^{\bullet}:(\Omega^{\bullet}_{A/R},d_{{\mathrm{dR}}})\rightarrow(B^{\bullet},d)
. In other words, the De Rham complex of
A
relative to
R
is the universal commutative differential graded
R
-algebra which has
A
as its degree
0
component.

First, let us start by clarifying what we mean by a derived divided power algebra. Let
\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the
\infty
-category of all
derived rings
as defined by A.Raksit
[
26
]
. The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is defined as the
\infty
-category of algebras over a certain monad

\mathrm{LSym}_{\mathbb{Z}}:\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}.

The monad
\mathrm{LSym}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is the unique monad which has the properties that its value on a finitely generated free abelian group on
n
generators is the polynomial ring
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},...,x_{n}]
, and that it preserves sifted colimits and finite totalizations. Recall that the free polynomial ring on an abelian group
M
can be defined more conceptually by the formula

\Sym_{\mathbb{Z}}(M):=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}(M^{\otimes^{n}})_{\Sigma_{n}},

where
(-)_{\Sigma_{n}}
stands for coinvariants with respect to the symmetric group, and the symmetric group acts on the tensor power
M^{\otimes^{n}}
by permutation.

The notion of a
divided power algebra
is obtained by replacing coinvariants with respect to symmetric groups by
invariants
, i.e. the free divided power algebra on
M
is given by the formula

\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(M):=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}(M^{\otimes^{n}})^{\Sigma_{n}}.

A more concrete description of the free divided power ring is as follows. Let us choose a basis
M=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Then
\mathbb{Z}\langle x_{1},...,x_{n}\rangle:=\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)
is the smallest subring of
\mathbb{Q}[x_{1},...,x_{n}]
which contains
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},...,x_{n}]
and all
divided powers
of the generators
x_{i}
, i.e. elements of the form

\frac{x_{i}^{d}}{d!}\in\mathbb{Q}[x_{1},...,x_{n}].

To promote the construction
M\longmapsto\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(M)
to a monad, one has to work with non-unital algebras
1
1

1

this has to do with the fact that outside of characteristic
0
, the unit in a unital ring never supports divided powers.
. The ring
\mathbb{Z}\langle x_{1},...,x_{n}\rangle
is augmented, and the augmentation ideal
\mathbb{Z}\langle x_{1},...,x_{n}\rangle^{+}
is a non-unital ring, which we call the
non-unital polynomial divided power ring
on
n
generators. We will denote
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{n}):=\mathbb{Z}\langle x_{1},...,x_{n}\rangle
. There exists the
non-unital derived divided power algebra
monad on
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, denoted

\mathrm{L\Gamma}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}:\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}},

which similarly to
\mathrm{LSym}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, is defined as the unique sifted colimit and finite totalization preserving monad whose value on a finitely generated free abelian group on
n
generators is
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
. We denote

\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathrm{nu},\mathrm{pd}}:=\Alg_{\mathrm{L\Gamma}^{+}_{\mathbb{Z}}}(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}})

the
\infty
-category of non-unital derived divided power rings. These definitions can be extended to any ground derived ring
R
, thus giving us the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
of derived
R
-algebras, and the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}^{\mathrm{nu},\mathrm{pd}}
of non-unital derived divided power
R
-algebras.

A global version of the notion of a non-unital derived divided power ring is the notion of a
derived divided power map
, or a
derived divided power Smith ideal
. Roughly, a derived divided power map
A\rightarrow B
is the data of a map of derived rings
A\rightarrow B
together with the data of a non-unital derived divided power
A
-algebra on
I=\fib(A\rightarrow B)
. To explain it more precisely, let us digress for a moment and delve into the notion of ideal in the derived world. Let
\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Delta^{1}}
be the
\infty
-category of arrows
A\rightarrow B
in the
\infty
-category of derived rings.
\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Delta^{1}}
is monadic over the arrow category
\mathrm{Mod}^{\Delta^{1}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Consider the functor
\fib:\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Delta^{1}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Delta^{1}}
sending an arrow
(X\rightarrow Y)
to the fiber
(\fib(X\rightarrow Y)\rightarrow X
. This functor is evidently an equivalence. Therefore, the data of a derived ring map
(A\rightarrow B)
can be equivalently characterized as the data of a derived ring
A
, together with a certain data on the map
I=\fib(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow A
. We refer to this data as the data of a
derived Smith ideal
, and explain in more details what it amounts to in the text. A part of the data of a Smith ideal
(I\rightarrow A)
is the structure of a non-unital derived
A
-algebra on
I
. We define a
derived divided power map

(A\rightarrow B)
as a map of derived rings
(A\rightarrow B)
, together with a lift of
I
to a non-unital derived divided power
A
-algebra. We call the corresponding derived Smith ideal
(I\rightarrow A)
a
derived divided power Smith ideal
, and sometimes simply
derived divided power algebra
for short. The notion can be extended to any ground derived ring
R
. We denote
\mathrm{DAlg}^{\Delta^{1},\mathrm{pd}}_{R}
the
\infty
-category of derived divided power maps over
R
, and reserve a separate notation
\mathrm{DAlg}^{\Delta^{1}_{\vee},\mathrm{pd}}_{R}
for the
\infty
-category of derived divided power Smith ideals over
R
. We freely interchange between the two notions, as they are equivalent, but use separate notations depending on whether we want to emphasize the data of a derived Smith ideal or not.

Furthermore, the theory of derived divided power Smith ideals admits a filtered analogue. Let
R
be a ground derived ring, and let
\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
be the
\infty
-category of non-negatively filtered objects in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, and
\Fil^{\geq 1}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\subset\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
the full subcategory of positive filtrations. We define an
\infty
-category of
non-negatively filtered derived
R
-algebras

\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
as the
\infty
-category of algebras over a certain filtered version
\mathrm{LSym}^{\geq\star}_{R}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
of the
\mathrm{LSym}
-monad. For any object
A^{\geq\star}\in\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
, the
0
-th stage of the filtration
A^{\geq 0}
is a derived
R
-algebra, and the
“augmentation fiber”

A^{\geq 1}
is a non-unital filtered derived
A^{\geq 0}
-algebra. Now the monad
\mathrm{L\Gamma}_{R}^{+}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
admits a filtered enhancement
\mathrm{L\Gamma}_{R}^{+,\geq\star}:\Fil^{\geq 1}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\Fil^{\geq 1}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, thereby giving as the notion of a
non-unital filtered derived divided power
R
-algebra
. We define an
\infty
-category
\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}
of
(non-negatively) filtered derived divided power
R
-algebras
as an
\infty
-category monadic over
\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, whose object consist of objects
A^{\geq\star}
in
\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
together with a
A^{\geq 0}
-linear non-unital filtered derived divided power structure on the augmentation fiber
A^{\geq 1}
. This monad is trivial in characteristic
0
, i.e. when
R
has characteristic
0
, the forgetful functor induces an equivalence
\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}^{\mathrm{pd}}\simeq\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
. In non-zero characteristic, the data of divided powers on a discrete object
A^{\geq\star}\in\Fil^{\geq 0}\CAlg^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R,\heartsuit}
roughly amounts to specifying for any element
x\in A^{\geq i}
in filtered stage
i\geq 1
a sequence of elements
\gamma_{n}(x)\in A^{\geq ni}
for all
n\geq 1
, which satisfy the usual divided power relations.

There is a functor of taking
0
-th graded piece
\gr^{0}:\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
. It is not easy to see that it preserves all limits and hence admits a left adjoint.

Definition 1.1.1
.

The left adjoint of the functor
\gr^{0}

\mathrm{L\Omega}_{-/R}^{\geq\star}:\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}

sends a derived
R
-algebra
A
to the
Hodge-filtered derived De Rham cohomology

\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\geq\star}_{A/R}
of
A
relative to
R
.

This definition requires justification. Recall that there already exists a functor
\mathbb{L}\Omega_{-/R}:\CAlg_{R,\heartsuit}\rightarrow\Fil^{\geq 0}\CAlg_{R}
with values in non-negatively filtered
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras obtained by resolving any
R
-algebra by polynomial algebras, and applying the functor of Hodge-filtered De Rham complex termwise. Extending this to all connective derived algebras by commuting with arbitrary geometric realizations, we obtain a functor
\mathbb{L}\Omega^{\geq\star}_{-/R}:\mathrm{DAlg}_{R,\geq 0}\rightarrow\Fil^{\geq 0}\CAlg_{R}
. To justify the Definition
1.1.1
, we need to show that the following triangle commutes

\textstyle{\mathrm{DAlg}_{R,\geq 0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}\Omega^{\geq\star}_{-/R}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\geq\star}_{-/R}}
\textstyle{\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Fil^{\geq 0}\CAlg_{R},}

(1.5)

where the right vertical functor is the forgetful functor. To prove this statement, we need a little more preliminary formalism. But already at this point, we notice that there is at least one immediate corollary of the definition.

Proposition 1.1.2
.

Let
R
be a discrete commutative ring, and
R\rightarrow A
be a surjective map such that the ideal
I=\ker(R\rightarrow A)
is generated by a regular sequence, and let
\Env^{\mathrm{pd},\geq\star}_{R}(I)
be the divided power envelope of
R
at
I
endowed with the divided power filtration. Then there is an equivalence of filtered
R
-algebras

\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\geq\star}_{A/R}\simeq\Env^{\mathrm{pd},\geq\star}_{R}(I).

To prove that the diagram
1.5
commutes, by commutation with sifted colimits it suffices to show that there is a natural equivalence
\mathrm{L\Omega}_{A/R}^{\geq\star}\simeq\Omega^{\geq\star}_{A/R}
for any polynomial
R
-algebra
A
. To formulate this comparison statement, we need to connect our definition of derived De Rham cohomology in terms of filtered divided power algebras with the more familiar definition of the De Rham cohomology as a cochain complex. Passing to the language of cochain complexes can be done as in
[
26
]
using the equivalence between the complete filtered derived category and the
\infty
-category of
dg modules
, or
coherent cochain complexes
. Namely, let
\widehat{\Fil\mathrm{Mod}}_{R}\subset\Fil\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
be the full subcategory of complete objects. There is a functor of taking
associated graded object

\gr:\widehat{\Fil\mathrm{Mod}}_{R}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathrm{Gr}\mathrm{Mod}_{R},

and it turns out that it is monadic. We let
\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
be the resulting monadic
\infty
-category over
\mathrm{Gr}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, so that the functor
\gr
lifts to an equivalence
\gr:\widehat{\Fil\mathrm{Mod}}_{R}\simeq\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}.
We define an
\infty
-category
\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{DAlg}^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}
of
derived differential graded divided power algebras
by requiring that the functor
\gr
lifts to an equivalence
\gr:\widehat{\Fil\mathrm{DAlg}}^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}\simeq\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{DAlg}^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}.

Definition 1.1.3
.

The functor of
derived De Rham complex

\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\bullet}_{A/R}:\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{DAlg}^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}

(1.6)

is the left adjoint of the limit preserving functor
\ev^{0}:\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{DAlg}^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
extracting the
0
-th degree part of a differential graded derived divided power algebra. Given a derived
R
-algebra
A
, the derived De Rham complex
\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\bullet}_{A/R}
can be computed as the composition
\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\bullet}_{A/R}:=\gr\;\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\geq\star}_{A/R}
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
has a so-called
negative
t
-structure
, whose heart is the abelian category of cochain complexes. What we precisely show is that for a smooth map
R\rightarrow A
, the derived De Rham complex
\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\bullet}_{A/R}
is discrete in this
t
-structure, and the corresponding cochain complex is the classical De Rham complex (with the structure of a commutative differential graded algebra). A precise formulation of this statement uses the décalage isomorphism of Illusie,
[
16
, Proposition 4.3.2.1]
.

Proposition 1.1.4
.

Let
R
be a discrete commutative ring, and
A
be a smooth
R
-algebra, and
\Omega^{\bullet}_{A/R}
be the De Rham complex of
A
over
R
. Then the derived De Rham complex
\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\bullet}_{A/R}
is discrete in the negative
t
-structure, and there is an equivalence of commutative differential graded
R
-algebras

\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\bullet}_{A/R}\simeq\Omega^{\bullet}_{A/R}.

Remark 1.1.5
.

At this point, let us clarify how the universal property given above is different from the Raksit’s. The dichotomy lies in what kind of algebras one considers as a target for the derived De Rham complex functor, and there are essentially two main points of difference.

- We have introduced an
\infty
-category
\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
such that the functor
\gr:\Fil\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
lifts to an equivalence
\gr:\widehat{\Fil\mathrm{Mod}}_{R}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.875pt\raise 4.34428pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.34428pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. In Raksit’s terminology, objects of
\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
are called
h_{-}
-differential graded objects
. There also exists a notion of
h_{+}
-differential graded objects
. Namely, following
[
26
]
, one defines a graded algebra
\mathbb{D}_{+}:=R\oplus R[1](1)
and the
\infty
-category of
h_{+}
-differential graded objects as
\mathrm{DG}_{+}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}:=\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{D}_{+}}(\mathrm{Gr}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
. Raksit gives a simple universal property of the derived De Rham complex which is analogous to the one given in Definition
1.1.3
, but in the setting of
h_{+}
-differential graded algebras, rather than in the setting of filtered derived algebras or
h_{-}
-differential graded algebras.

- The theory of the derived De Rham complex in the setting of
h_{+}
-differential graded algebras turns out to be very different from the setting of filtered derived algebras or
h_{-}
-differential graded algebras. In the
h_{+}
-setting, there are no divided powers. In contrast, in the filtered/
h_{-}
-setting, divided powers become crucial. The main reason why one needs divided powers, is that the left adjoint of the functor
\ev^{0}:\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
is
not
the derived De Rham complex, but is rather related to the
infinitesimal cohomology
.

One important property of (derived) De Rham cohomology is its relation to the notions of the universal derivation and the universal square-zero extension. This can be explained by means of the following two examples. Let
R
be a discrete commutative ring, and
A
a smooth
R
-algebra. Then the naive truncation
\Omega_{A/R}^{\{0,1\}}
of the De Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}_{A/R}
in degrees
\leq 2
coincides with the two-step complex
d:A\rightarrow\Omega_{A/R}^{1}
, where the map
d
is the
universal (De Rham) derivation
of the map
R\rightarrow A
. its universality is that for any other
R
-linear derivation
\delta:A\rightarrow M
with values in an
A
-module
M
, there is a unique map
f:\Omega^{1}_{A/R}\rightarrow M
such that the derivation
\delta
factors as
\delta=f\circ d
. Another example is the case of a surjective map
R\rightarrow A
whose ideal
I
is generated by a regular sequence. As we mentioned above, there is an equivalence
\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\geq\star}_{A/R}\simeq\Env_{R}^{\geq\star}(I)
. Truncating the latter filtered object in degrees
\leq 2
, we obtain a square-zero extension

I/I^{2}\rightarrow R/I^{2}\rightarrow R/I.

This square-zero extension also has a universal property. Suppose we have a map
(I\rightarrow R)\rightarrow(J\rightarrow S)
of algebras with an ideal, and
J^{2}=0
. Then this map factors uniquely through a map
(I/I^{2}\rightarrow R/I^{2})\rightarrow(J\rightarrow S)
. For this reason, we might call the object
I/I^{2}\rightarrow R/I^{2}
the
universal square-zero extension
associated to the ideal
I\subset R
. The two examples we just explained suggest two ideas:

1) truncation of the derived De Rham cohomology (or derived De Rham complex) in degrees
\leq 2
must have a universal property as a universal square-zero extension (respectively, the universal derivation),

2) there should exist a canonical way of transforming the universal square-zero extension into the universal derivation, and vice versa.

The second idea is in fact an instance of one of the foundational ideas of derived algebraic geometry: square-zero extensions are controlled by derivations. The passage from square-zero extensions to derivations is done by taking associated gradeds for two-step filtration existing on any square-zero extension, and interpreting the extension data as a derivation. A reasonable expectation then is that the equivalence of square-zero extensions and derivations is a specialization of the equivalence
\widehat{\Fil\mathrm{Mod}}_{R}\simeq\mathrm{DG}_{-}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
to objects concentrated in degrees
0,1
only. Indeed, consider subcategories
\Fil^{[0,1]}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\subset\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
of
two-step filtrations
and
\mathrm{DG}_{-}^{\{0,1\}}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\subset\mathrm{DG}^{\geq 0}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
of
differential two-step graded modules
. We show that
\Fil^{[0,1]}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
and
\mathrm{DG}_{-}^{\{0,1\}}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
are symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories and there is a symmetric monoidal equivalence

\Fil^{[0,1]}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\simeq\mathrm{DG}_{-}^{\{0,1\}}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}.

Moreover, we interpret the
\infty
-category
\Fil^{[0,1]}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}:=\mathrm{DAlg}(\Fil^{[0,1]}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
as the
\infty
-category of
derived square-zero Smith ideals
and
\mathrm{DG}_{-}^{\{0,1\}}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}:=\mathrm{DAlg}(\mathrm{DG}_{-}^{\{0,1\}}\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
as the
\infty
-category of
derived derivations
. For instance, objects of the latter can be thought as triples
(A,M,\delta)
where
A\in\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
,
M\in\mathrm{Mod}_{A}
, and
\delta:A\rightarrow A\oplus M[1]
is a derivation. There exists a symmetric monoidal functor

\textstyle{\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gr}
\textstyle{\mathrm{DG}_{-}^{\geq 0}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\{0,1\}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{DG}_{-}^{\{0,1\}}\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}

which induces a functor
\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{DG}^{\{0,1\}}_{-}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
, which produces a universal derivation for any non-negatively filtered derived algebra. We show the following:

Theorem 1.1.6
.

Let
R\rightarrow A
be a map of derived rings, and
\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\geq\star}_{A/R}
be the Hodge filtered derived De Rham cohomology of
A
over
R
. Then the differential two-step graded object
\mathrm{L\Omega}^{\{0,1\}}_{A/R}
is canonically equivalent to the universal derivation
d:A\rightarrow A\oplus\mathbb{L}_{A/R}
.

This statement is to be expected, and its proof rather tautologically follows by checking universal properties. The essential content of the discussion above is Theorem
3.3.26
which interprets the equivalence
\Fil^{[0,1]}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}\simeq\mathrm{DG}_{-}^{\{0,1\}}\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
as an equivalence between square-zero Smith ideals and derivations.

In the end of the text, we explain how to set-up the theory of derived crystalline cohomology relative to a general (derived) divided power base. We consider the
\infty
-category
\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathrm{crys}}}
consisting of pairs
(A\rightarrow\overline{A},R)
where
(A\rightarrow\overline{A})
is a derived divided power map (to which we will refer to as
divided power base
), and
\overline{R}
a derived
\overline{A}
-algebra, and define a functor
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{crys}}}:\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathrm{crys}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Delta^{1},\mathrm{pd}}
with values in the
\infty
-category of derived divided power rings, whose value on a pair
(A\rightarrow\overline{A},R)
is the
derived crystalline cohomology of
R
relative to
(A\rightarrow\overline{A})
, denoted
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{crys}}}(R/(A\rightarrow\overline{R})
. We show that it follows from our definition that derived crystalline cohomology satisfies a base change property with respect to maps
(A\rightarrow\overline{A})\rightarrow(B\rightarrow\overline{B})
of divided power bases. This property implies the following theorem, which summarizes the main properties of derived crystalline cohomology. To formulate it, we notice that for a given divided power base
(A\rightarrow\overline{A})
, the derived divided power ring
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{crys}}}(R/(A\rightarrow\overline{A}))
is a derived divided power algebra under
(A\rightarrow\overline{A})
, and hence, n particular, the underlying derived ring of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{crys}}}(R/(A\rightarrow\overline{A}))
is a derived
A
-algebra.

Theorem 1.1.7
.

Let
(A\rightarrow\overline{A})
be a derived divided power base.

1.

Let
R
be a derived
\overline{A}
-algebra. There is a natural equivalence

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{crys}}}(R/(A\rightarrow\overline{A}))\underset{A}{\otimes}\overline{A}\simeq\mathrm{L\Omega}_{R/\overline{A}}.

2.

Let
\overline{R}
be a derive
\overline{A}
-algebra, and
R
a derived
A
-algebra endowed with an equivalence
R\otimes_{A}\overline{A}\simeq\overline{R}
. Then there is a natural equivalence

\mathrm{L\Omega}_{R/A}\simeq\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{crys}}}(\overline{R}/(A\rightarrow\overline{A})).

3.

Let
(\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\overline{A})
be the derived divided power envelope of
(A\rightarrow\overline{A})
. Recall that
\mathcal{A}\simeq\mathrm{L\Omega}_{\overline{A}/A}
. The divided power structure on
(A\rightarrow\overline{A})
gives a natural map
(\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\overline{A})\rightarrow(A\rightarrow\overline{A})
. Let
R
be a derived
\overline{A}
-algebra, and consider its derived De Rham cohomology
\mathrm{L\Omega}_{R/A}
with the natural
\mathcal{A}
-linear structure. There is a natural equivalence

\mathrm{L\Omega}_{R/A}\underset{\mathcal{A}}{\otimes}A\simeq\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{crys}}}(R/(A\rightarrow\overline{A})).

The equivalences listed above are formulated as equivalences of derived algebras. In the relevant section, we explain how to promote it to an equivalence of derived divided power algebras.

The first two properties are the main properties expected of crystalline cohomology. The first merely states, that derived crystalline cohomogy is a natural lift of derived De Rham cohomology. The second says that we can compute derived crystalline cohomology as derived De Rham cohomology of a lift. The third property is a generalization of the idea that one can compute the crystalline cohomology of an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra
R
as the derived De Rham cohomology of
R
relative to
\mathbb{Z}
, based changed to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
along the natural map
\mathrm{L\Omega}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. This observation is to the best of our knowledge, due to B.Bhatt, and appears in the text
[
4
]
.

Let us finish this introduction by mentioning the comparison with the stacky and site-theoretic approach, which will be proven in a subsequent paper
[
21
]
. In the work of
[
9
]
of Drinfeld, and the works
[
2
]
and
[
5
]
of B.Bhargav and J.Lurie there was developed a stacky approach to derived De Rham cohomology. Let
X
be a derived stack over a base ring
R
. We can define a new prestack
X_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
, called
De Rham stack
of
X
, understood merely as a fuctor
\mathrm{DAlg}_{R,\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spc}
which is defined by the formula

X_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(R):=X(R/R^{\sharp}),

where
R/R^{\sharp}
is the quotient of
R
by a certain generalized ideal
R^{\sharp}\rightarrow R
playing the role of nil-radical in the divided power setting. One can show (see
[
5
]
, Theorem 2.5.6) that for a smooth affine scheme
X
, there is an equivalence of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}

R
-algebras:

\Gamma(X_{{\mathrm{dR}}},\mathcal{O}_{X_{{\mathrm{dR}}}})\simeq|\Omega^{\bullet}_{A/R}|.

More generally, it follows from
[
2
, Theorem 7.20]
and the crystalline comparison for prismatic cohomology that the statement holds true for the derived crystalline cohomology either when the algebra satisfies some finiteness assumption, or is quasi-lci. It is expected that the result should be true for derived De Rham cohomology for any
A
, if we replace the right-hand side by Hodge-completed derived De Rham cohomology. At present we are not aware of a reference for this statement proven in this generality. In the paper
[
21
]
, we characterize the construction
X\longmapsto\Gamma(X_{{\mathrm{dR}}},\mathcal{O}_{X_{{\mathrm{dR}}}})
in terms of a universal property which makes manifest the fact that for any affine scheme
X
, the
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra
\Gamma(X_{{\mathrm{dR}}},\mathcal{O}_{X_{{\mathrm{dR}}}})
lifts to a filtered derived divided power algebra in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. Let
\Fil^{\geq\star}\Gamma(X_{{\mathrm{dR}}},\mathcal{O}_{X_{{\mathrm{dR}}}})
be the object of
\Fil^{\geq 0}\mathrm{DAlg}^{\mathrm{pd}}_{R}
arising from this construction. In
[
21
]
we will prove the following:

Theorem 1.1.8
.

Let
A
be a connective derived commutative
R
-algebra, and
X=\Spec A
. There is a natural filtered map

\textstyle{\mathrm{L\Omega}_{A/R}^{\geq\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Fil^{\geq\star}\Gamma(X_{{\mathrm{dR}}},\mathcal{O}_{X_{{\mathrm{dR}}}})}

which induces an equivalence on Hodge completions.

As we mentioned above, the isomorphism above holds for derived De Rham cohomology without Hodge completion for any map
R\rightarrow A
satisfying either a simple finiteness condition, or a certain quasi-lci condition. We do not know whether the statement is true without Hodge completion for any map
R\rightarrow A
, but if a counter-example exists, it has to be some infinitely-generated and not quasi-lci algebra.

1.2
Notations and terminology.

We use the language of
\infty
-categories and higher algebra developed by J.Lurie in
[
19
]
and
[
20
]
. We freely use the notions of an
\infty
-category
, a
functor between
\infty
-categories
, an
\infty
-categorical adjunction
etc. The amount of higher categorical machinery used in the text, is limited to a very basic level. Essentially the only technical statements we use are the
adjoint functor theorem

[
19
, Corollary 5.5.2.9]
, and the
Barr-Beck-Lurie monadicity theorem
formulated in
[
20
, Theorem 4.7.3.5]
. In general, most of our constructions are formulated in a homotopy invariant way. In particular, all limits and colimits are in general assumed to be in the homotopy sense. For better exposition, we often restrict our attention to some discrete computations, in which case we specify that we are working with discrete cochain complexes, algebras etc.

We let
\mathrm{Spc}
be the
\infty
-category of
spaces
, or
\infty
-groupoids
, and
\mathrm{Spt}
the
\infty
-category of
spectra
. For a ring spectrum
A
, we denote
\mathrm{Mod}_{A}
the
\infty
-category of
A
-module spectra
. For example, our notation for the derived
\infty
-category of abelian groups is
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. In a stable
\infty
-category
\mathrm{C}
, we denote
[1]
and
[-1]
suspension and loop functors respectively.

We often encounter pull-back diagrams of
\infty
-categories. Unless specified otherwise, these are taken in the
\infty
-category of large
\infty
-categories.

For a pair of
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{C}
and
\mathrm{D}
, we let
\Fun(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D})
be the
\infty
-category of functors from
\mathrm{C}
to
\mathrm{D}
. The
\infty
-category
\Fun(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{C})
has a monoidal structure given by composition of functors. A
monad on
\mathrm{C}
is an associative algebra object in this monoidal
\infty
-category.

Most
\infty
-categories we will encounter in the text will be presentable. We let
\Pr^{L}
to be the
\infty
-category of presentable
\infty
-categories and continuous functors. This
\infty
-category has a symmetric monoidal structure constructed by J.Lurie.

A
presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
is a commutative algebra object in
\Pr^{L}
.

We use the
\bullet
-symbol for the grading on a graded object
X^{\bullet}
, and the
\star
-symbol to indicate the filtration on a filtered object
X^{\geq\star}
.

We use homological notation in the context of
t
-structures on a stable
\infty
-category. For instance, for a stable
\infty
-category
\mathrm{C}
with a
t
-structure,
\mathrm{C}_{\geq 0}
is the full subcategory of connective objects. For the full subcategory of discrete objects, we use the symbol
\mathrm{C}_{\heartsuit}
.

Given a presentable
\infty
-category
\mathrm{C}
, we denote
\mathrm{C}^{\omega}\subset\mathrm{C}
the full
\infty
-subcategory consisting of compact objects in
\mathrm{C}
.

Given a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{C}
, one can talk about
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra objects in
\mathrm{C}
. For us,
\mathrm{C}
will typically be a stable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of the form
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
for
R
a derived commutative ring. In this setting, we can also talk about
derived commutative
R
-algebras
.

In the context of derived algebraic geometry, we follow some basic terminology of the texts
[
11
]
,
[
12
]
. For instance, we use the notions of
prestack
and
quasicoherent sheaves on prestacks
. A prestack over a derived ring
R
is a functor
X:\mathrm{DAlg}_{R,\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spc}
, where
\mathrm{DAlg}_{R,\geq 0}
is the
\infty
-category of connective commutative
R
-algebras over the base ring
R
. For an
A\in\mathrm{DAlg}_{R,\geq 0}
, there is a representable prestack
\Spec(A)
, i.e.
a derived affine scheme
. Any prestack
X
can be written as a colimit of derived affine schemes

X=\underset{\Spec(A)\rightarrow X}{\colim}\;\;\Spec(A)

The
\infty
-category of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
is the limit

\QCoh(X)\simeq\underset{\Spec(A)\rightarrow X}{\lim}\;\;\mathrm{Mod}_{A}

taken along the diagram of all derived affine schemes mapping to
X
using base change functors
-\underset{A}{\otimes}B:\mathrm{Mod}_{A}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{B}
for any map
\Spec(B)\rightarrow\Spec(A)
over
X
.

[
22

foundation.

2
Preliminaries.

2.1
Higher category theory.

Here we will review some higher categorical facts used in the text. We will often work with various
\infty
-categories built as pull-backs and/or lax equalizers of other symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories. We recall the following construction from Nikolaus-Scholze
[
24
, Definition II.1.4]
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

Assume given two
\infty
-categoties
\mathrm{C}
and
\mathrm{D}
and two functors
F,G:\mathrm{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
. The
Lax equalizer

\infty
-category
\mathrm{LEq}(F,G)
is defined as the pull-back

\textstyle{\mathrm{LEq}(F,G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{D}^{\Delta^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\partial^{0},\partial^{1})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F,G)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{D}\times\mathrm{D}}

(2.1)

We will often study the case
\mathrm{D}=\mathrm{C}
,
F=\Id
, and
G
is some endofunctor of
\mathrm{C}
. Moreover, we will use the fact that if
\mathrm{C}
is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category and
G
is a lax symmetric monoidal functor, then
\mathrm{LEq}(\Id,G)
gets a symmetric monoidal structure. Moreover, commutative algebra objects in
\mathrm{LEq}(\Id,G)
are precisely pairs
(A,f)
where
A
is a commutative algebra object in
\mathrm{C}
, and
f:A\rightarrow G(A)
is a map of commutative algebra objects. To explain this, let us recall some basic terminology regarding symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories. Let
\mathrm{Fin}_{*}
be the category of finite pointed sets. In the approach of J.Lurie, an
\infty
-operad
is a functor
p:\mathcal{O}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fin}_{*}
satisfying conditions listed in
[
20
, Definition 2.1.1.10]
. A
symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
is an
\infty
-category
\mathrm{C}^{\otimes}
equipped with a coCartesian fibtration
\mathrm{C}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fin}_{*}
of
\infty
-operads. A
lax symmetric monoidal functor

F:\mathrm{C}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\otimes}
is simply an
\infty
-operad map. Let
\CAlg(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}
be the
\infty
-category of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories and lax symmetric monoidal functors. There is a fully faithful embedding
\CAlg(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}\subset\mathrm{Op}_{\infty}
, where
\mathrm{Op}_{\infty}
is the
\infty
-category of operads defined as in
[
20
, Definition 2.1.4.1]
. Given a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
p:\mathrm{C}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fin}_{*}
, we let
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}_{\langle 1\rangle}:=p^{-1}(\langle 1\rangle)
. The functor
\CAlg(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
,
(p:\mathrm{C}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fin}_{*})\longmapsto\mathrm{C}
is conservative and preserves all limits
2
2

2

Caution: the inclusion
\CAlg(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Op}_{\infty}
is, however, not closed under all limits.
.

In the future, we will often write the data of a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
p:\mathrm{C}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fin}_{*}
simply as
\mathrm{C}
keeping symmetric monoidal structure in mind. The tautological coCartesian fibration
\mathrm{Fin}_{*}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fin}_{*}
is the
commutative operad
. For a given symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{C}:=(p:\mathrm{C}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fin}_{*})
, the collection of all operad maps
\mathrm{Fin}_{*}\rightarrow\mathrm{C}^{\otimes}
organize into an
\infty
-category of
commutative algebra objects
in
\mathrm{C}
, denoted
\CAlg(\mathrm{C})
. The following proposition follows immediately from definitions.

Proposition 2.1.2
.

Assume we are given

\textstyle{\mathrm{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\underset{i}{\lim}\;\mathrm{C}_{i}}

(2.2)

a limit diagram in
\CAlg(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}
. Then the natural map in
\mathrm{Cat}

\textstyle{\CAlg(\mathrm{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\underset{i}{\lim}\;\mathrm{C}_{i}.}

is an equivalence.

Proposition 2.1.3
.

Assume
\mathrm{C}
and
\mathrm{D}
are symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories,
F
is a symmetric monoidal functor, and
G
is lax symmetric monoidal. The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{LEq}(F,G)
has a symmetric monoidal structure, and there is an equivalence

\CAlg(\mathrm{LEq}(F,G))\simeq\mathrm{LEq}(F,G:\CAlg(\mathrm{C})\rightarrow\CAlg(\mathrm{D})).

Proof.

Using
[
24
, Construction IV.2.1 (ii)]
, there is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{LEq}(F,G)^{\otimes}
defined as a pull-back diagram of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories and lax symmetric monoidal functors, whose underlying
\infty
-category is
\mathrm{LEq}(F,G)
. Taking the
\infty
-category of commutative algebra objects and using
2.1.2
, we get a pull-back square of
\infty
-categories

\textstyle{\CAlg(\mathrm{LEq}(F,G))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\CAlg(\mathrm{D}^{\Delta^{1}})\simeq\CAlg(\mathrm{D})^{\Delta^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(ev^{0},\ev^{1})}
\textstyle{\CAlg(\mathrm{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F,G)}
\textstyle{\CAlg(\mathrm{D}\times\mathrm{D})\simeq\CAlg(\mathrm{D})\times\CAlg(\mathrm{D}),}

and the second statement follows.
∎

Remark 2.1.4
.

Note that if
F
is only lax symmetric monoidal, but not strictly symmetric monoidal, there is no natural symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathrm{LEq}(F,G)
. This is an example demonstrating that in general the
\infty
-category
\CAlg(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}
is not closed under all limits inside
\mathrm{Op}_{\infty}
. It is crucial that when
F
is strictly symmetric monoidal, one can construct a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathrm{LEq}(F,G)
making the square
2.1
a square of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories and lax symmetric monoidal functors. In other words, the commutative square
2.2
needs to be
given
, and in general, we
can not define

\mathrm{C}
as a pull-back of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories and lax symmetric monoidal functors.

Over a ring of non-zero characteristic, in addition to
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras, there are also theories of derived commutative algebras as well as derived divided power algebras. We will review some basics of derived commutative algebras following
[
26
]
in the next subsection, and the theory of derived divided power algebras in Section 2.3 via the approach of
[
6
]
. We will also formulate and prove a version of Proposition
2.1.2
in the setting of derived algebras in Section 2.3. To this end, we will introduce the abstract framework in which the theories of derived commutative algebras and derived divided power algebras fit.

Recall that given an
\infty
-category
\mathrm{C}
, a
monad
on
\mathrm{C}
is an algebra object in the monoidal
\infty
-category
\Fun(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{C})
with respect to the composition product. To formulate and prove a version of Proposition
2.1.2
in the setting of algebras over a monad, we ought to have, given a pair of
\infty
-categories
(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}})
and
(\mathrm{D},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{D}})
endowed with monads, the notion of a
lax equivariant
functor
F:(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}})\rightarrow(\mathrm{D},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{D}})
with respect to these monads action. In order to speak about such lax equivariant functors, we use some basic
(\infty,2)
-categorical formalism. We generally follow the approach of
[
11
]
to
(\infty,2)
-categories, and use some elements of the formalism developed by R.Haugseng in the text
[
14
]
. We will give references for all definitions and statements, but will not present a detailed overview of the formalism and restrict to simply explaining what various constructions mean on the naive level of 2-categories.

Notation 2.1.5
.

We denote
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
the
(\infty,1)
-category of
(\infty,2)
-categories. There exist several approaches to
(\infty,2)
-categories in the literature. For instance, one can think of objects of
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
as the
\infty
-categories of
\infty
-categories enriched in the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Cat}
. This means that an
(\infty,2)
-category
C
is characterized by the data of a collection of objects
X\in\textbf{C}
and for any pair
X,Y\in\textbf{C}
, an
(\infty,1)
-category
\Hom(X,Y)
together with composition functors

\Hom(X,Y)\times\Hom(Y,Z)\rightarrow\Hom(X,Z)

with a hierarchy of higher coherences
3
3

3

See
[
14
, Remark 2.14]
for discussion and references to this approach. We do not have to choose a specific model, because most our statements and constructions already exist in the literature, and we only need to reference them and apply to concrete problems.
. The elements of
\Hom(X,Y)
are usually called
1-morphisms
in
C
, and morphisms of
\Hom(X,Y)
are usually called
2-morphisms
in
C
. We use bold letters like
\textbf{C},\textbf{D},...
for objects of
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
. Given an
(\infty,2)
-category
C
, we denote
\imath_{1}\textbf{C}
the underlying
(\infty,1)
-category obtained by discarding all non-invertible
2
-morphisms. We denote
Cat
the
(\infty,2)
-category of
(\infty,1)
-categories
4
4

4

We usually call
(\infty,1)
-categories simply
“
\infty
-categories”
, unless there is any possibility of confusion, or unless we want to be extra pedantic.
. The construction of
Cat
uses the natural enhancement of the usual
(\infty,1)
-category of
(\infty,1)
-categories by letting the
\Hom
-category between two objects
\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D}\in\mathrm{Cat}
to be the
\infty
-category
\Fun(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D})
of all functors and not necessarily invertible natural transformations. Given two
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D}
, there is an
(\infty,1)
-category
\Fun(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D})
whose objects are functors
F:\mathrm{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
, and morphisms are not necessarily invertible natural transformations
F\Rightarrow G
.

Construction 2.1.6
.

Given a pair of
(\infty,2)
-categoies
\textbf{C},\textbf{D}
, there is an
\infty
-category
\Fun(\textbf{C},\textbf{D})_{\mathrm{lax}}
of 2-functors
\textbf{C}\rightarrow\textbf{D}
and
lax natural transformations
. A lax natural transformation
\eta:F\Rightarrow G
between 2-functors
F,G:\textbf{C}\rightarrow\textbf{D}
is the data assigning to any 1-morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
C
a lax square

\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}}
\textstyle{G(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G(f)}
\textstyle{F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{Y}}
\textstyle{G(Y),}

i.e. a not necessarily invertible map
\eta_{f}:G(f)\circ\eta_{X}\rightarrow\eta_{Y}\circ F(f)
. Dually, there is also a notion of an
oplax natural transformation
for two functors
F,G:\textbf{C}\rightarrow\textbf{D}
.

See
[
14
, Definition 2.9]
for a precise definition of
\Fun(\textbf{C},\textbf{D})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}
using Gray tensor product. We will give a very short overview here. There exists a bifunctor
\otimes^{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}:\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}\times\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
called
(op)lax Gray tensor product
which preserves colimits in each variable, and such that for any triple of
(\infty,2)
-categories
\textbf{C},\textbf{D},\textbf{E}
, we have

\Map_{\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}(\textbf{C},\Fun(\textbf{D},\textbf{E})_{\mathrm{op}\mathrm{lax}})\simeq\Map_{\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}(\textbf{D}\otimes^{\mathrm{op}\mathrm{lax}}\textbf{C},\textbf{E})

\simeq\Map_{\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}(\textbf{C}\otimes^{\mathrm{lax}}\textbf{D},\textbf{E})\simeq\Map_{\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}(\textbf{D},\Fun(\textbf{C},\textbf{E})_{\mathrm{lax}}).

Unwinding the definition, one obtains that an (op)lax natural transformation between functors
F,G:\textbf{C}\rightarrow\textbf{D}
is a functor of
(\infty,2)
-categories
\textbf{C}\otimes^{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\textbf{D}
whose restriction to
0,1\in\Delta^{1}
is
F
and
G
respectively.

Example 2.1.7
.

Let
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,...\}
be the monoid of natural numbers by addition. We will apply the previous construction to the case
\textbf{C}:=\mathrm{B}\mathbb{N}
, which is an ordinary category with one object and a unique non-identity morphism, and
\textbf{D}:=\textbf{Cat}
. The data of a functor
\mathrm{B}\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}
is the same as the data of a pair
(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{T}_{C})
where
\mathrm{C}
is an
\infty
-category, and
\mathrm{T}:\mathrm{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{C}
an endofunctor. Giving a lax natural transformation
F:(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}})\rightarrow(\mathrm{D},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{D}})
is equivalent to giving a functor
F:\mathrm{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
and for any
X\in\mathrm{C}
a lax equivariant structure map
\eta_{X}:\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{D}}\circ F(X)\rightarrow F\circ\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}(X)
.

Construction 2.1.8
.

This construction follows
[
14
, Definition 4.2, Definition 5.2]
and the references therein.

1.

There exist a universal
2
-category
mnd
containing a monad. The universal property of
mnd
is that giving a functor
\textbf{mnd}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}
is the same as giving an
\infty
-category
\mathrm{C}
endowed with a monad
\mathrm{T}:\mathrm{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{C}
. The
2
-category
mnd
contains a single object with
\hom
-category being the augmented simplex category, i.e.
\textbf{mnd}\simeq\mathrm{B}\Delta_{a}
. We define the
(\infty,2)
-category of monads and (op)lax-equivariant 1-morphisms
in
C
as

\mathrm{Mnd}(\textbf{cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}:=\Fun(\textbf{mnd},\textbf{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}.

To unravel the definition above, objects of
\mathrm{Mnd}(\mathrm{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}
are pairs
(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}})
consisting of an
\infty
-category
\mathrm{C}
endowed with a monad
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}
and maps are functors
F:\mathrm{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
which are (op)lax-equivariant with respect to the monads
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}
and
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{D}}
. We also denote
\mathrm{Mnd}(\mathrm{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}:=\imath_{1}\Fun(\textbf{mnd},\textbf{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}
for simplicity.

More generally, the notion of a monad makes sense internally to any
(\infty,2)
-category. Given
\textbf{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
, we define the
(\infty,2)
-category of monads and (op)lax equivariant
1
-morphisms in
C
as the
(\infty,2)
-category of functors
\mathrm{Mnd}(\textbf{C})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}:=\Fun(\textbf{mnd},\textbf{C})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}.

2.

There exits a universal
2
-category
adj
containing an adjunction with the universal property that the data of a functor
\textbf{adj}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}
is equivalent to the data of a pair of
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D}
endowed with an adjunction
\textstyle{\mathrm{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{C}}
\scriptstyle{F}
. We define the
(\infty,2)
-category of adjunctions and (op)lax-equivariant functors
as

\mathrm{Adj}(\textbf{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}:=\Fun(\textbf{adj},\textbf{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}},

and use the simplified notation
\mathrm{Adj}(\mathrm{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}:=\imath_{1}\Fun(\textbf{adj},\textbf{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}
for the underlying
(\infty,1)
-category. There exists a forgetful functor
\mathrm{Adj}(\textbf{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mnd}(\textbf{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}
sending any adjunction
\textstyle{\mathrm{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{C}}
\scriptstyle{F}
to the monad
(\mathrm{C},G\circ F)
.

The notion of an adjunction is well-defined internally to any
(\infty,2)
-category. Given
\textbf{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
, the
\infty
-category of adjunctions and (op)lax equivariant
1
-morphisms in
C
is by definition, the
(\infty,2)
-category of functors
\mathrm{Adj}(\textbf{C}):=\Fun(\textbf{adj},\textbf{C})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}.

3.

According to
[
14
, Corollary 5.8]
, the forgetful functor
\mathrm{Adj}(\textbf{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mnd}(\textbf{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}
admits a fully faithful right adjoint
\mathrm{Mnd}(\textbf{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Adj}(\textbf{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}
sending an object
(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{T})\in\mathrm{Mnd}(\textbf{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}
to the adjunction

\textstyle{\Alg_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathrm{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U_{\mathrm{T}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{C},}
\scriptstyle{F_{\mathrm{T}}}

where
\Alg_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathrm{C})
is the
\infty
-category of
\mathrm{T}
-algebras in
\mathrm{C}
,
F_{\mathrm{T}}
is the free algebra functor, and
U_{\mathrm{T}}
is the forgetful functor. The claim that
\Alg_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathrm{C})
is the conventional
\infty
-category of
\mathrm{T}
-algebras in
\mathrm{C}
as defined in
[
20
]
is established in
[
14
, Proposition 8.10]
.

Proposition 2.1.9
.

Assume
(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.875pt\raise 4.34428pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.34428pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\underset{i}{\lim}(\mathrm{C}_{i},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}})
is a limit diagram in
\mathrm{Mnd}(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}
. In this situation, the natural functor induces an equivalence:

\textstyle{\Alg_{\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}}(\mathrm{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\underset{i}{\lim}\Alg_{\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}}(\mathrm{C}_{i})}

Proof.

This follows immediately from part 3 of Construction
2.1.8
as right adjoints commute with limits, and the functor
\mathrm{Adj}(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
sending an adjunction
\textstyle{\mathrm{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{C}}
\scriptstyle{F}
to the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{D}
(the source of the right adjoint) commutes with limits as well.
∎

Construction 2.1.10
.

Let
\mathrm{A}
be a monoidal
\infty
-category,
BA
the corresponding
(\infty,2)
-category which has a single object with
\hom
-category being
\mathrm{A}
. We let

\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{A}}(\textbf{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}:=\Fun(\textbf{BA},\textbf{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}

be the
(\infty,2)
-category of
(\infty,1)
-categories with an
\mathrm{A}
-action and (op)lax
\mathrm{A}
-equivariant functors between them
, and following our usual convention let
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}:=\imath_{1}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{A}}(\textbf{Cat})_{(\mathrm{op})\mathrm{lax}}
. Giving an algebra object
\mathrm{T}\in\Alg(\mathrm{A})
is equivalent to giving a functor of
(\infty,2)
-categories
\textbf{mnd}\rightarrow\textbf{BA}
. Consequently, given a monad
\mathrm{T}
in
\Alg(\mathrm{A})
, we get a limit-preserving restriction functor

\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathrm{Mnd}(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}

(2.3)

which takes any
\infty
-category
\mathrm{C}
with
\mathrm{A}
-action to the monad
(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}})
, where
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}
is the image of
\mathrm{T}\in\Alg(\mathrm{A})
under the monoidal functor
\mathrm{A}\rightarrow\Fun(\mathrm{C},\mathrm{C})
.

We shall now recall the construction of passing to adjoints in the
(\infty,2)
-categorical setting, and how it interchanges oplax and lax natural transformations. Given a pair of
(\infty,2)
-categories
\textbf{C},\textbf{D}
, let
\Fun(\textbf{C},\textbf{D})_{\mathrm{oplax},L}
be the subcategory of
\Fun(\textbf{C},\textbf{D})_{\mathrm{oplax}}
with all maps being left adjoint functors, and similarly
\Fun(\textbf{C},\textbf{D})_{\mathrm{lax},R}
the subcategory of
\Fun(\textbf{C},\textbf{D})_{\mathrm{lax}}
with all maps being right adjoint functors.
[
12
, Ch.12, Corollary 3.1.9]
states that taking adjoints gives a natural equivalence

\Fun(\textbf{C},\textbf{D})_{\mathrm{oplax},L}\simeq\Fun(\textbf{C}^{(1,2)-\mathrm{op}},\textbf{D})_{\mathrm{lax},R},

where the superscript
{(1,2)-\mathrm{op}}
stands for inverting all
1
and
2
-morphisms.

Applying this general fact to the construction
2.3
, we get a commutative diagram of
\infty
-categories

\textstyle{(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{Cat})_{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathrm{Mnd}(\mathrm{Cat})_{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax},R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Mnd}(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax},R},}

where the vertical functors are taking right adjoints of
1
-morphisms.

We will use the following Proposition in several crucial places.

Proposition 2.1.11
.

Assume
\mathrm{A}
is a monoidal
\infty
-category with a fixed algebra object
\mathrm{T}\in\Alg(\mathrm{C})
. Suppose that we are given a diagram
\{\mathrm{C}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{Cat})_{L}
such that the corresponding diagram in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}
obtained by passing to right adjoints, has a limit
\mathrm{C}\simeq\underset{i}{\lim}\;\mathrm{C}_{i}
in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{Cat})_{\mathrm{lax}}
. Then Construction
2.1.10
produces an equivalence

\textstyle{\Alg_{\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}}(\mathrm{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\underset{i}{\lim}\Alg_{\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}}(\mathrm{C}_{i}).}

2.2
Non-abelian derived functors.

In this section we will review some basic ideas from the theory of derived commutative rings. Many ideas of this subsection are due to L.Brantner and A.Mathew and were first developed in the paper
[
7
]
in the case
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{Mod}_{k}
,
k
a field. We will closely follow the terminology of the paper of A.Raksit
[
26
]
.

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let
\CAlg^{\mathrm{poly}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the category of all finitely generated polynomial rings. The
\infty
-category of
connective derived commutative rings

\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z},\geq 0}
is obtained by freely adjoining all sifted colimits to
\CAlg^{\mathrm{poly}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z},\geq 0}:=\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\CAlg^{\mathrm{poly}}_{\mathbb{Z}}).

Given an object
R
, we let
\mathrm{DAlg}_{R,\geq 0}:=(\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z},\geq 0})_{R/}
be the under category of
connective derived commutative
R
-algebras
.

Remark 2.2.2
.

The strategy of defining
R
-algebras used above will be used very frequently throughout the text. In many cases, we will define some
\infty
-category of derived rings over
\mathbb{Z}
first, and then define
R
-linear objects as an under-category. Consequently, many constructions with derived rings of different types can be done in the case
R
is a discrete ring, or even
R=\mathbb{Z}
, and then generalized to under-objects.

Remark 2.2.3
.

Let
R
be a discrete commutative ring. The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
of left
R
-modules is a stable presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category. There is a
t
-structure
(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0},\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\leq 0})
characterized by the property that
M\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is connective if the underlying spectrum is connective. This
t
-structure is compatible with the symmetric monoidal structure and is right and left complete. In addition, there is a full subcategory
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}
of finitely generated free modules which is closed under direct sums and tensor powers. This
\infty
-category compactly and projectively generates
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}
, i.e.
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}\simeq\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0})
.

We will assume that
R
is discrete throughout this subsection, unless explicitly stated.

The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{DAlg}_{R,\geq 0}
is monadic over
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}
. It turns out that it is possible to extend this monad to a monad on
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, so that one gets the free
derived commutative algebra
monad
\mathrm{LSym}_{\mathrm{R}}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. The paper
[
26
]
performs this construction by using the natural filtration on the free symmetric algebra. We will briefly review the Raksit’s construction below.

Example 2.2.4
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. The symmetric power of
X
is defined by the formula

\Sym_{\mathrm{C}}(X):=(\otimes^{n}X)_{h\Sigma_{n}},

where the subscript
(-)_{h\Sigma_{n}}
denotes homotopy orbits with respect to the symmetric group action. The functor
\Sym^{\leq n}=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\Sym^{i}_{\mathrm{R}}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
is excisively polynomial of degree
n
. The (discrete) symmetric power of
X\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\heartsuit}
is defined as

\Sym_{R,\heartsuit}(X):=(\otimes^{n}X)_{\Sigma_{n}},

where the coinvariants are taken in the naive sense. The functors
\Sym^{\leq n}_{R,\heartsuit}:=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\Sym^{i}_{R,\heartsuit}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\heartsuit}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\heartsuit}
are additively polynomial of degree
n
.

Construction 2.2.5
.

Let
\mathrm{T}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
a functor. We define
the right-left Kan extension
of
\mathrm{T}
,
\mathrm{T}^{RL}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
as the right Kan extension along the inclusion
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}\subset\mathrm{Mod}^{\omega}_{R,\leq 0}
, followed by the left Kan extension to all of
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
.

Definition 2.2.6
.

Let
\mathrm{D}
be an
\infty
-category containing all totalizations and geometric realizations. We say that a functor
G:\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\leq 0}^{\omega}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
preserves
finite coconnective geometric realizations
if for any simplicial object
A_{\ast}
in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\leq 0}^{\omega}
which is
n
-skeletal for some
n
such that the geometric realization
|A_{\ast}|
belongs to
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\leq 0}^{\omega}
, the colimit of
G(A_{\ast})
exists in
\mathrm{D}
and the natural map
|G(A_{\ast})|\rightarrow G(|A_{\ast}|)
is an equivalence. A functor
G:\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\mathrm{free},\mathrm{fg}}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
is
right-left extendable
if the right Kan extension
RG:\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\leq 0}^{\omega}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
preserves finite coconnective geometric realizations.

Definition 2.2.7
.

This definition concerns the two notions of polynomiality for functors of
\infty
-categories.

•

Let
\mathrm{C}
be an additive
\infty
-category, and
\mathrm{D}
an
\infty
-category containing small colimits. An additively polynomial functor
F:\mathrm{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
of degree
0
is a constant functor. Assume a polynomial functor of degree
n-1
has been defined. A functor
F:\mathrm{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
is
additively polynomial of degree
n
if the derivative
DF_{X}(Y)=\fib(F(X\oplus Y)\rightarrow F(X))
is of degree
n-1
. A functor
F
is
additively polynomial
if it is additively polynomial of some degree.

•

Let
\mathrm{C}
be an
\infty
-category containing finite colimits, and
\mathrm{D}
an
\infty
-category containing all limits. A functor
F:\mathrm{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
is
excisively polynomial
if it is
n
-excisive of some degree
n
in the sense of Goodwillie calculus (see
[
26
, Definition 4.2.7]
).

We let
\Fun_{\mathrm{addpoly}}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0},\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
be the
\infty
-category of additively polynomial functors
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
and
\Fun_{\mathrm{excpoly}}^{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R},\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
be the
\infty
-category of excisively polynomial sifted colimit preserving functors
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
.

For future reference, we record the following result proven in
[
7
, Proposition 3.34, Theorem 3.35]
in the case
R
is a field, and generalized to an arbitrary derived algebraic context in
[
26
, Proposition 4.2.14, Proposition 4.2.15]
.

Proposition 2.2.8
.

Let
R
be a connective derived commutative ring. The restriction functor

\textstyle{\Fun^{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{excpoly}}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R},\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Fun_{\mathrm{addpoly}}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0},\mathrm{Mod}_{R})}

is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories with inverse given by right-left extension.

Remark 2.2.9
.

For any right-left extendable functor
\mathrm{T}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
, the restriction
\mathrm{T}^{RL}|_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}}
of its right-left extension to the full subcategory of connective objects coincides with the left Kan extension of
F
along the inclusion
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}
. In this case, we will use the notation
\mathrm{LT}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}
for the derived functor defined on all
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
.

Definition 2.2.10
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\times}
be the category of non-negative integers considered with monoidal structure given by multiplication. A
filtered (sifted colimit preserving) monad
is a lax monoidal functor

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{\leq\star}:\mathbb{Z}^{\times}_{\geq 0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\End_{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}).}

(2.4)

A.Raksit shows in
[
26
]
that for a filtered monad
\mathrm{T}^{\leq\star}
, the colimit
\mathrm{T}:=\underset{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}{\colim}\;\mathrm{T}^{\leq\star}
is a monad on
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. The advantage of using filtered monads is that many monads are not excisively polynomial on the nose, but have filtrations whose stages are excisively polynomial functors.

Example 2.2.11
.

The functor

\textstyle{\Sym_{R}^{\leq\star}:\mathbb{Z}^{\times}_{\geq 0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\End_{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})}

(2.5)

\textstyle{n\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(X\longmapsto\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\Sym^{i}_{R}(X))}

is a filtered monad such that
\underset{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}{\colim}\Sym_{R}^{\leq\star}\simeq\Sym_{R}
. Moreover, the stages of the filtration are sifted colimit preserving excisively polynomial functors. It follows that the functor
\Sym_{R}^{\leq\star}
lands in the full subcategory
\End_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{excpoly}}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})\subset\End(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}),
which is equivalent to
\Fun^{\mathrm{addpoly}}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0},\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
by Proposition
2.2.8
. In other words, the filtered monad
\Sym_{\mathrm{R}}^{\leq\star}
is right-left extended from
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. As it preserves the connective subcategory
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, the monad
\Sym_{R}^{\leq\star}
is in particular, uniquely determined by the filtered monad
\Sym^{\leq\star}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}}
on
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}
.

Construction 2.2.12
.

Let
R
be a
discrete
commutative ring. Let
\Sym:\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
be the free commutative algebra functor,
\Sym_{R}(M):=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}(M^{\otimes^{n}})_{\Sigma_{n}}
. Construction
2.2.5
supplies a derived functor
\mathrm{LSym}_{R}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
which we will refer to as the
free derived commutative algebra
in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. Then
[
26
, Construction 4.2.19]
shows that the functor
\mathrm{LSym}_{R}\in\End^{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
has a monad structure. This is achieved via a version of Example
2.2.11
in the derived setting. Let
\End^{\Sigma}_{0}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0})
be the full subcategory of
\End^{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
consisting of functors which preserve the full subcategory
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}
, and
\End^{\Sigma}_{1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0})
the full subcategory of functors satisfying
\pi_{0}F(X)\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}
for any
X\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R,0}
. Raksit shows in
[
26
, Remark 4.2.18]
that the inclusion
\End_{0}^{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0})\subset\End_{1}^{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0})
admits a
monoidal
left adjoint
\tau:\End^{\Sigma}_{1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0})\rightarrow\End^{\Sigma}_{0}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0})
. As we observed in Example
2.2.11
, the filtered monad
\Sym^{\leq\star}_{R}
is uniquely determined by its restriction to the connective subcategory
\mathrm{Mod}_{R,\geq 0}\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. Moreover, for any
n
, the functor
\Sym^{\leq n}_{R}
lies in
\End_{1}^{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R})
. We can define a new filtered monad
\mathrm{LSym}^{\leq\star}_{R}
by the formula
\mathrm{LSym}^{\leq\star}_{R}:=\tau(\Sym_{R}^{\leq\star})
. Moreover, it is an excisively polynomial filtered monad, therefore it extends to a filtered monad on all of
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. Defining

\mathrm{LSym}_{R}:=\underset{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}{\colim}\;\mathrm{LSym}_{R}^{\leq\star},

we get a monad on
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
.

Definition 2.2.13
.

Let us apply the Construction
2.2.12
to the initial ring
R=\mathbb{Z}
.
A derived commutative ring
is an algebra over the monad
\mathrm{LSym}_{\mathbb{Z}}:\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}
from Construction
2.2.12
. We let
\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}}:=\Alg_{\mathrm{LSym}_{\mathbb{Z}}}(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}})
to be the
\infty
-category of derived commutative rings. The forgetful functor
\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\CAlg_{\mathbb{Z}}
commutes with all limits and colimits. Given any other (at this point, not necessarily discrete) object
R\in\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we define the
\infty
-category of
derived commutative
R
-algebras
as the under category

\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}:=(\mathrm{DAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}})_{R/}.

The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{DAlg}_{R}
is monadic over
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
with the monad
\mathrm{LSym}_{R}:\mathrm{Mod}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
which satisfies the formula

\mathrm{LSym}_{R}(V\otimes R)\simeq\mathrm{LSym}_{\mathbb{Z}}(V)\otimes R

for any induced
R
